Mostly it’s the thinking-through part, I suppose. I wouldn’t want to overstate it, but… my mind just will not hold onto things the way it used to.

Yes… yes, I have. I’ve been trying not to make too much of it, because I know everyone has low spells, but most days I wake up with this… heavy, hollow feeling in my chest.

For example, how often do they happen, and have they affected things like your work, daily tasks, or relationships?\n\nPatient: It happens… more than I’d like, I suppose. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program — something I really could have done in my sleep before — and the numbers and words just sort of scatter on me. I look at the page and can’t seem to keep it all lined up in my head.\n\nI’m not sure if that answers it properly. It’s just… it has started getting in the way of things I used to manage pretty easily.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these memory problems? I don’t know that I’d say it’s only recent events.\n\nIt’s like when I sit down to write even a simple note for the youth maker program — something I really should be able to do without much effort — the words and numbers just scatter on me. I can’t keep them lined up long enough to get them down properly.\n\nI’m sorry, I’m not sure if that answers the difference you’re asking about. I can’t seem to keep them in order long enough.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as organizing or finding words exactly, but that’s what I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your thinking or abilities besides memory, such as trouble speaking clearly, finding the right words, making decisions, or physical coordination?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s so much my speech or coordination… at least, I haven’t noticed that clearly. I’m… still managing some things, I think, but not as well as I’d like to say.

I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program — something I really could have done in my sleep before — and the numbers and words just sort of scatter on me. It’s just… it has started getting in the way of things I used to manage pretty easily.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these memory problems? I can’t seem to keep them in order long enough.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as organizing or finding words exactly, but that’s what I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your thinking or abilities besides memory, such as trouble speaking clearly, finding the right words, making decisions, or physical coordination?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s so much my speech or coordination… at least, I haven’t noticed that clearly. For example, how often do they happen, and have they affected things like your work, daily tasks, or relationships?\n\nPatient: It happens… more than I’d like, I suppose. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s become hard to ignore.\n\nThe main thing is, my mind just won’t hold onto things the way it used to. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program — something I really could have done in my sleep before — and the numbers and words just sort of scatter on me. I look at the page and can’t seem to keep it all lined up in my head.\n\nI’m not sure if that answers it properly. It’s just… it has started getting in the way of things I used to manage pretty easily.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these memory problems? When did you first notice them?\n\nPatient: I think… about three weeks now. It wasn’t really something I marked on a calendar, but I first noticed it after my older sister died. Since then it’s just been harder to keep things straight in my head.\n\nI hope that’s the sort of timeline you mean.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if the memory problems you're having mostly involve forgetting things that happened recently, or do you also have trouble learning and remembering new information as well?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I can’t… hold things in my head properly, if that makes sense. I don’t know that I’d say it’s only recent events.\n\nIt’s like when I sit down to write even a simple note for the youth maker program — something I really should be able to do without much effort — the words and numbers just scatter on me. I can’t keep them lined up long enough to get them down properly.\n\nI’m sorry, I’m not sure if that answers the difference you’re asking about. It just feels like my mind won’t hold onto things anymore.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your ability to remember things and handle tasks like writing notes has declined from how it used to be in the past?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it has declined. I don’t like saying that, because I used to be able to manage those things pretty well.\n\nI came in because my mind just doesn’t seem to hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program — something I honestly could’ve done in my sleep before — and the words and numbers just sort of scatter on me. It’s… not like me.\n\nIs that the kind of change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your thinking besides memory, such as trouble finding the right words when speaking, difficulty planning or organizing, or problems with coordination or balance?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the thinking-through part, I suppose. I wouldn’t want to overstate it, but… my mind just will not hold onto things the way it used to.\n\nLike with the youth maker program, I’ll sit down to write a simple note — something I really could’ve done almost without thinking before — and the numbers and words just scatter on me. I can’t seem to keep them in order long enough.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as organizing or finding words exactly, but that’s what I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your thinking or abilities besides memory, such as trouble speaking clearly, finding the right words, making decisions, or physical coordination?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s so much my speech or coordination… at least, I haven’t noticed that clearly. It’s more that my mind just doesn’t seem to hold onto things anymore.\n\nI know I’ve said it, but I came in because even writing a simple note for the youth maker program has become hard. That used to be something I could do in my sleep, and now the numbers and words just sort of scatter on me. I can’t keep them together long enough.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as decision-making or organizing, but that’s the change I’m noticing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble with things like understanding or using language, solving problems, or recognizing objects or shapes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m having trouble recognizing objects or shapes… that sounds a bit stronger than what I’ve noticed.\n\nIt’s more that my mind just won’t hold onto things the way it used to. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program — something I really could do in my sleep before — and the numbers and words just sort of scatter on me. I can’t seem to keep them together long enough to make it come out right.\n\nDoes that count as problem-solving or language trouble, do you think?\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to think through problems or make decisions lately, even simple ones?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s really why I came in.\n\nMy mind just doesn’t seem to hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program — something I honestly could’ve done in my sleep before — and the numbers and words just scatter on me. I can’t keep them lined up long enough to make it come out right.\n\nSo yes, I suppose even simple things feel harder to think through now. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt confused or disoriented, for example, about where you were, what day it was, or what you were doing?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not in the sense of not knowing where I am or what day it is. I haven’t had anything like that that I can point to.\n\nI mean, I may lose my train of thought, but I’m not getting disoriented like that. At least, I don’t believe I am. Is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble recognizing familiar places, judging distances, or telling the difference between colors or shapes?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had trouble with that. Familiar places look familiar to me, and I’m not mixing up colors or shapes as far as I can tell.\n\nJudging distances… I mean, nothing I’ve noticed in a real way. I’d say that part seems okay. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program — something I really could have done in my sleep before — and the numbers and words just sort of scatter on me. It’s just… it has started getting in the way of things I used to manage pretty easily.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these memory problems? I don’t know that I’d say it’s only recent events.\n\nIt’s like when I sit down to write even a simple note for the youth maker program — something I really should be able to do without much effort — the words and numbers just scatter on me. I can’t seem to keep them in order long enough.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as organizing or finding words exactly, but that’s what I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your thinking or abilities besides memory, such as trouble speaking clearly, finding the right words, making decisions, or physical coordination?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s so much my speech or coordination… at least, I haven’t noticed that clearly. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program — something I honestly could’ve done in my sleep before — and the numbers and words just scatter on me. I can’t keep them lined up long enough to make it come out right.\n\nSo yes, I suppose even simple things feel harder to think through now. That used to be something I could do in my sleep, and now the numbers and words just sort of scatter on me. I can’t keep them together long enough.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as decision-making or organizing, but that’s the change I’m noticing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble with things like understanding or using language, solving problems, or recognizing objects or shapes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m having trouble recognizing objects or shapes… that sounds a bit stronger than what I’ve noticed.\n\nIt’s more that my mind just won’t hold onto things the way it used to. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program — something I really could do in my sleep before — and the numbers and words just sort of scatter on me. I can’t seem to keep them together long enough to make it come out right.\n\nDoes that count as problem-solving or language trouble, do you think?\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to think through problems or make decisions lately, even simple ones?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s really why I came in.\n\nMy mind just doesn’t seem to hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program — something I honestly could’ve done in my sleep before — and the numbers and words just scatter on me. I can’t keep them lined up long enough to make it come out right.\n\nSo yes, I suppose even simple things feel harder to think through now. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt confused or disoriented, for example, about where you were, what day it was, or what you were doing?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not in the sense of not knowing where I am or what day it is. I haven’t had anything like that that I can point to.\n\nI mean, I may lose my train of thought, but I’m not getting disoriented like that. At least, I don’t believe I am. Is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble recognizing familiar places, judging distances, or telling the difference between colors or shapes?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had trouble with that. Familiar places look familiar to me, and I’m not mixing up colors or shapes as far as I can tell.\n\nJudging distances… I mean, nothing I’ve noticed in a real way. I’d say that part seems okay. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood lately, like feeling sad, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I’ve been trying not to make too much of it, because I know everyone has low spells, but most days I wake up with this… heavy, hollow feeling in my chest.\n\nAnd even when my wife is being kind to me, talking gently, I still feel very far away from it somehow. Like I’m sitting at the bottom of a well and she’s up above me. I don’t mean that dramatically — that’s just the closest way I can describe it.\n\nSo yes, sad, I suppose. Low. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did the memory and thinking problems begin clearly within three months of your sister’s death, or do you think they started later than that?\n\nPatient: It was… pretty soon after. I’d say within that time, yes. It’s been about three weeks now, really since my older sister died.\n\nI don’t know if I’m describing it the right way, but that’s when I started noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these memory and thinking problems are keeping you from doing things you normally do on your own, like managing finances, driving, or following a conversation, or are you still able to manage those things with just a little more effort?\n\nPatient: I’m… still managing some things, I think, but not as well as I’d like to say.\n\nI haven’t really kept up with my church responsibilities, and the garden has more or less gone to seed. There’s also the youth maker program I helped with… I’ve let that fall away too. My wife and the pastor have had to step in more than I’m comfortable admitting.\n\nThe workshop is the hardest one to say out loud. I don’t really feel safe being in there by myself right now, so I’ve been avoiding it unless someone’s around. I suppose that’s more than just “a little more effort,” isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your difficulties with thinking and memory are affecting most areas of your daily life, or are they mostly limited to specific tasks like writing notes?\n\nPatient: I suppose… I’d like to say it’s just specific tasks, like writing notes. That sounds less worrying.\n\nBut honestly, that’s what brought me in — my mind just doesn’t seem to hold onto things the way it used to. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I could have done almost without thinking before, and the words and numbers just… scatter on me.\n\nSo maybe it starts with those little tasks, but it feels bigger than that when I’m trying to do them. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your thinking feels slowed down or foggy most of the time, even when you're not trying to remember something specific?\n\nPatient: Yes… foggy is probably the right word. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but my mind just doesn’t seem to hold onto things anymore.\n\nEven something simple, like sitting down to write a note for the youth maker program — something I used to do almost without thinking — the words and numbers just scatter on me. I keep trying to pull it together, but it’s… it’s not like it used to be. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I can’t seem to keep them together long enough to make it come out right.\n\nDoes that count as problem-solving or language trouble, do you think?\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to think through problems or make decisions lately, even simple ones?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s really why I came in.\n\nMy mind just doesn’t seem to hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program — something I honestly could’ve done in my sleep before — and the numbers and words just scatter on me. I can’t keep them lined up long enough to make it come out right.\n\nSo yes, I suppose even simple things feel harder to think through now. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt confused or disoriented, for example, about where you were, what day it was, or what you were doing?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not in the sense of not knowing where I am or what day it is. I haven’t had anything like that that I can point to.\n\nI mean, I may lose my train of thought, but I’m not getting disoriented like that. At least, I don’t believe I am. Is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble recognizing familiar places, judging distances, or telling the difference between colors or shapes?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had trouble with that. Familiar places look familiar to me, and I’m not mixing up colors or shapes as far as I can tell.\n\nJudging distances… I mean, nothing I’ve noticed in a real way. I’d say that part seems okay. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood lately, like feeling sad, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I’ve been trying not to make too much of it, because I know everyone has low spells, but most days I wake up with this… heavy, hollow feeling in my chest.\n\nAnd even when my wife is being kind to me, talking gently, I still feel very far away from it somehow. Like I’m sitting at the bottom of a well and she’s up above me. I don’t mean that dramatically — that’s just the closest way I can describe it.\n\nSo yes, sad, I suppose. Low. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did the memory and thinking problems begin clearly within three months of your sister’s death, or do you think they started later than that?\n\nPatient: It was… pretty soon after. I’d say within that time, yes. It’s been about three weeks now, really since my older sister died.\n\nI don’t know if I’m describing it the right way, but that’s when I started noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these memory and thinking problems are keeping you from doing things you normally do on your own, like managing finances, driving, or following a conversation, or are you still able to manage those things with just a little more effort?\n\nPatient: I’m… still managing some things, I think, but not as well as I’d like to say.\n\nI haven’t really kept up with my church responsibilities, and the garden has more or less gone to seed. There’s also the youth maker program I helped with… I’ve let that fall away too. My wife and the pastor have had to step in more than I’m comfortable admitting.\n\nThe workshop is the hardest one to say out loud. I don’t really feel safe being in there by myself right now, so I’ve been avoiding it unless someone’s around. I suppose that’s more than just “a little more effort,” isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your difficulties with thinking and memory are affecting most areas of your daily life, or are they mostly limited to specific tasks like writing notes?\n\nPatient: I suppose… I’d like to say it’s just specific tasks, like writing notes. That sounds less worrying.\n\nBut honestly, that’s what brought me in — my mind just doesn’t seem to hold onto things the way it used to. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I could have done almost without thinking before, and the words and numbers just… scatter on me.\n\nSo maybe it starts with those little tasks, but it feels bigger than that when I’m trying to do them. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your thinking feels slowed down or foggy most of the time, even when you're not trying to remember something specific?\n\nPatient: Yes… foggy is probably the right word. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but my mind just doesn’t seem to hold onto things anymore.\n\nEven something simple, like sitting down to write a note for the youth maker program — something I used to do almost without thinking — the words and numbers just scatter on me. I keep trying to pull it together, but it’s… it’s not like it used to be. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your difficulties are severe enough that you need help from others to handle things like paying bills, preparing meals, or keeping track of your schedule?\n\nPatient: I… I wouldn’t say I need help with everything, exactly. I’m still trying to keep hold of what I can.\n\nBut yes, some things have slipped more than I’d like to admit. I’ve let my church responsibilities go, and the garden too… and the youth maker program. My wife and the pastor have had to step in for me.\n\nAnd the workshop… I don’t really feel safe being in there by myself right now, so I avoid it unless someone’s around. I suppose that does mean I need more help than I want to say. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current level of thinking and memory is significantly worse compared to how you were functioning a year ago?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is worse than a year ago. I don’t like saying that, because maybe some of it is just normal aging, I don’t know.\n\nBut I came in because my mind just will not hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program — something I could have done in my sleep before — and the numbers and words just scatter on me.\n\nSo… yes, compared to last year, it feels significantly worse. Is that the sort of comparison you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
